II. 
Assessment of performance against the investment criteria  
2.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
            
 
  Scale: High 
9. 
Brazil’s continuous work on improving their forest monitoring have led to in combination 
with the implementation of various policies and measures to the remarkable result of reducing 6.1 
billion t CO2 eq of emission from deforestation for the period of 2006-2015. This high volume of 
results matches approximately 50% of all CO2 emissions from all Annex I countries in 2012 alone. 
Because of the implementation of policies and measures in the Amazon biome, the LULUCF sector is 
no longer the highest emitting sector in Brazil.  
10. 
Brazil’s Action plan for the prevention and control of deforestation in the legal Amazon 
(PPCDAm) has served as the main vehicle since 2004 in achieving these results. The plan is now 
entering its 4th phase operating until 2020. The PPCDAm has through its innovative approach of 
focusing on land use, territorial planning, fostering sustainable production, environmental 
monitoring, control and enforcement managed to support Brazil’s efforts in combatting 
deforestation, and as a result, Brazil has reported the most ambitious results in the history of the 
UNFCCC. In parallel to all this, Brazil y has managed to combat and manage leakage 
11. 
The total volume of emissions reductions achieved by Brazil in this period, including those 
proposed to the REDD-PLUS results-based payments pilot programme (2014-2015), are all 
included in the technical annexes to the two biennial update reports submitted to the UNFCCC. 
These results were technically assessed and verified in accordance with COP decisions on REDD-
PLUS.   
12. 
It is therefore considered that the results achieved provide significant global benefits in 
mitigating climate change. 
2.2 
Paradigm shift potential     
 
 
 
    
 
Scale: High 
13. 
The implementation of REDD-plus policies and measures has furthermore had an important 
paradigm shift impact by contributing to the protection of forests but maintaining an upward trend 
in agricultural production within years of lower deforestation rates. Although deforestation trends 
have increased in recent years, the project will reinvest in important areas such as governance 
structures and a forest policy which are expected to further help the Amazon region to continue 
reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome. It is expected that the payments 
provided through the pilot programme for REDD+ results-based payments as well as the

=== PAGE 116 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 116 
 
 
 
 
 
contributions from several donors may incentivize the public and private sector to return to the 
path taken over more than a decade of increasing rural economy, securing indigenous rights and 
reducing deforestation.     
2.3 
Sustainable development potential  
 
 
             Scale: High  
14. 
REDD-PLUS policies in Brazil have since 2006 contributed strongly to economic, 
environmental and social sustainability. The proceeds paid for by the GCF are expected to 
contribute directly to continue these efforts. The use of proceeds for piloting the forest policy and 
implement the national REDD-PLUS strategy on governance issues should fully lead to sustainable 
development through better protection and conservation of forests, improved governance 
structures, improved engagement of local communities and thereby improved livelihoods. In 
addition, the proposed project is expected to engage and improve the livelihoods of women who are 
mostly in disadvantaged positions.  
15. 
The implementation of the PPCDAm has over the years brought more attention to the 
communities of the Amazon region through its many social policies and projects. This proposal will 
support this through implementation of the PPCDAm.  
16. 
Brazil has progressed its gender agenda significantly over the years. Programmes such as 
Bolsa Familia, Bolsa Verde target sustainable development with gender issues playing a central 
role.  Many of these lessons learned will be incorporated into the Floresta+ which will be supported 
through this project.  
17. 
Brazil will use its proceeds to continue its investments and strong commitment to combat 
and reduce deforestation in strengthening its governance and providing incentives to the 
stakeholders. This is reflected by its commitment to invest in an Environmental Services Incentive 
Program for Conservation and Recovery of Native Vegetation and through the support the National 
REDD-plus Strategy, Brazil continues to commit to combat deforestation. And that reinvestment of 
the proceeds will continue to support Brazil’s PPCDAm and in achieving continuous results from 
reducing emissions from deforestation.  
2.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
 
          Scale: High 
18. 
There are large number of indigenous peoples from several different ethnic groups and 
rural population living in the Brazilian Amazon, which also includes some of the most vulnerable 
population with little or none contact with modern societies.  Several reports and published 
literature make reference to the improvement of economic growth in the Brazilian Amazon, while 
at the same time notice increased inequality and poverty of original settlers2.      
19. 
GCF’s support will allow Brazil to distribute the payments directly to the rural population 
living in the Amazon as part of the implementation of the Floresta + program. Not to mention that 
the project will help Brazil pilot new innovative approaches for results-based payments that has the 
potential to attract and catalyse the private sector and other donors and investors with the 
objective to reduce the current deforestation trends.  
2.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
             
      
 Scale: High 
20. 
The project is in full alignment with Brazil’s NDC, REDD-PLUS strategy as well as national 
policies and strategies. The project is furthermore in full alignment with the strategic areas of 
                                                     
2 https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3426830/

=== PAGE 117 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 117 
 
 
 
 
 
investment of the Brazil’s country program to the GCF. The PPCDAm has since 2004 been the main 
vehicle to combat deforestation and continues to lead the way.  
21. 
Stakeholder engagement has been embedded in REDD-PLUS in Brazil since 2009, before 
safeguards were formally defined by the UNFCCC in 2010. Since then there has been numerous 
seminars, meetings and workshops to discuss the role and participation of indigenous peoples. In 
2016 a national safeguards advisory board was established to oversee safeguards in the Brazilian 
context.  
22. 
The Ministry of Environment has played a key role in the preparation of the funding 
proposal, leading the discussions on use of proceeds and with regards to policy issues. The Ministry 
is the responsible entity for coordinating the implementation of the PPCDAm since it was launched 
in 2004. The proposal is therefore fully aligned with national priorities.  
2.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
 
Scale: High 
23. 
The REDD-plus results for 2014 and 2015 presented to GCF and the suggested budget 
clearly highlight that a fixed price of USD 5 per tCO2eq will be applied to the project, which is cost 
effective and in accordance with the terms of reference of the pilot programme. Furthermore, the 
payment for these past results is reinvested into a transformation of the forest sector, which will 
allow Brazil to continue combatting deforestation.  
24. 
The reinvestment of the use of proceeds has the potential to attract other financial support 
through its innovative approach and opportunities for piloting the incentivizing forest policy. This 
will not only help support the Amazon biome but be applicable to the entire country and other 
countries in the amazon basin.  
25. 
The numerous lessons learned can furthermore help Brazil combat deforestation in other 
regions and continue to inspire and share lessons learned with other countries in the Amazon Basin 
through the south-south collaboration set up by the Amazon Fund.  
III. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
3.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
26. 
The Government of Brazil through the accredited entity (AE) is submitting a proposal for 
the payment of REDD-plus actions undertaken in 2014 and 2015 as part of Brazil’s Plan for the 
Prevention and Control of Deforestation in the Legal Amazon (PPCDAm). In meeting the 
requirements of GCF’s pilot programme for the REDD-plus results-based payments, the AE has 
conducted a review of the safeguards implementation of the period for which payment is requested 
and an environmental and social due diligence on the plans for the use of results-based payments. 
An Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) was prepared that describes the extent to which 
the actions that generated the results were undertaken in a manner consistent with the ESS 
standard of GCF. The AE also provided an Environmental and Social Screening Report identifying 
the environmental and social risks and potential impacts of activities that will be supported by the 
payment of results and an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) that 
describes the due diligence and management of these risks and impacts.  
27. 
The PPCDAm started in 2004 and is among the key programs of Brazil to reduce 
deforestation in the country. As a multiagency and multisectoral programme, the programme is 
coordinated by the Ministry of Environment and has a three-pillared strategy: (i) territorial and 
land use planning; (ii) environmental control and monitoring; and (iii) fostering sustainable 
production activities. The PPCDAm is implemented in a phased approach with the results period 
implemented in the third phase. The Amazon Fund was established in 2008 to mobilize resources

=== PAGE 118 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 118 
 
 
 
 
 
to support efforts prevent, monitor and address deforestation as well as promote the conservation 
and sustainable management of the Amazon biome. The Amazon Fund is one of the key financial 
instruments supporting the implementation of the PPCDAm. The Amazon Fund follows the REDD-
PLUS Social and Environmental Safeguards, with specifications developed for Brazil through 
stakeholder consultations and is also subject to the environmental and social due diligence of the 
fund manager Banco Nacional de Desenvolvimento Economico e Social (BNDES).     
28. 
Developing and implementing REDD-plus activities in Brazil requires, among others, that 
the activities address and respect the UNFCCC safeguards (Cancun safeguards) as described in 
Decision 1/CP.16. For Brazil, this also entailed interpreting the Cancun safeguards according to the 
country’s context and based on the interpretation formed the basis of the assessment oh how each 
safeguard was addressed and respected throughout the implementation of the activities. The 
elaboration of the interpretation and the report on how the safeguards were addressed and 
respected is contained in the country’s Summary of Information (SOI).      
29. 
The ESA presents the review of the PPCDAm focusing on the extent of consistency or 
alignment with the GCF ESS standards, particularly of the following: (i) policies, laws and 
regulations (PLRs); (ii) the application of the PLRs at the project or activity level; and (iii) 
stakeholder engagement and grievance redress in relation to the PPCDAm and the activities 
supported. The analytical framework for the ESA developed by the AE is underpinned by an 
alignment study of the policies and actions to establish consistency with the AE’s Social and 
Environmental Standards (SES), considered by the AE to be coherent with the GCF ESS standards. 
The alignment study utilized specific screening questions and standards covering various thematic 
safeguards issues. The screening questions included those related to the following SES principles: 
(i) human rights, encompassing risks and benefits related to indigenous peoples, property and land 
tenure, benefits sharing, access to information, and grievance redress; (ii) gender equality and 
women’s empowerment; and (iii) environmental sustainability.  The review concludes that during 
the period of 2014 to 2015 for which the results were generated, and payment is sought, the 
PPCDAm indicates a good level of alignment with the AE’s SES and in particular found consistency 
with the key objectives of the SES without significant shortcomings. The ESA identified aspects of 
the PLR that can benefit from future improvements or from other elements of the national REDD-
plus strategy. However, even with such aspects, the ESA concludes positive alignment with the AE’s 
SES. 
30. 
In terms of stakeholder engagement, the ESA indicates a good level of stakeholder 
participation in REDD-plus activities. This is expected given the previous efforts of the government 
and stakeholders in developing the national REDD-plus strategy and other REDD-plus activities. 
Furthermore, the governance structure of the PPCDAm provides for representation of various 
stakeholders including indigenous peoples and other vulnerable population. However, the ESA 
notes that the development of a grievance redress system associated with the national REDD-plus 
strategy is ongoing with consideration being given to establishing an Ombudsman function that 
would advocate for the REDD-plus safeguards.   
31. 
The ESA also reviewed how the policies were applied at the project level with the view to 
understand how the implementation of the project activities was consistent with the AE’s SES. 
Three projects were considered in the ESA representing the projects supported by the Amazon 
Fund during the third phase of the PPCDAm and include (i) Going Green Project; (ii) Catalyzing the 
Contribution of Indigenous Lands to the Conservation of Brazil’s Forest Ecosystems; and (iii) 
Amazon Portal Seeds Project. The review utilized indicators aligned with the SES overarching policy 
and standards. The ESA concludes that the project safeguards, aligned with regulatory, policy and 
institutional framework contributed to the effective implementation of the PPCDAm and showed 
consistency with the AE’s SES.  There are however identified aspects of project safeguard 
implementation that would need to be improved such as (i) gender mainstreaming including 
gender analysis and gender action plan of projects; (ii) establishment of project feedback and 
grievance redress mechanism; (iii) broadening the scope of environmental and social assessment

=== PAGE 119 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 119 
 
 
 
 
 
and due diligence; and (iv) strengthening the capacity of implementing entities to promote and 
improve participation and engagement of indigenous peoples and other vulnerable populations.  
32. 
E&S risk category. The Accredited Entity has classified the RBP project as having likely 
moderate environmental and social (E&S) risks and impacts, equivalent to Category B of the GCF 
project safeguard categorization. Preliminary screening undertaken by the Accredited Entity shows 
that the E&S risks from activities related to incentives that will be provided to conserve native 
vegetation and prevent deforestation for output 1 may be moderate. Further screening will be 
undertaken after defining and designing the project’s activities. Activities or components identified 
as having high E&S risks will be excluded from the project.  
33. 
Safeguard instruments prepared for the project (including their disclosure). The Accredited 
Entity has submitted an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) as the main 
safeguards document for the project. The activities of the project are yet to be specified fully in 
terms of specific locations and components, and therefore the ESMF has been prepared to set out 
the guidelines and procedures for screening, assessing, and managing the potential E&S impacts 
particularly those related to output 1. Clarification is needed the type of safeguard activity or 
documents that will be required at each stage of the subprojects or activities and the agencies 
responsible. Once the activities or components are finalized and with details, the ESMF will need to 
be updated to include a subproject or activity screening checklist. The checklist will need to be 
developed to assess the eligibility of the proposed subproject or activity, and if eligible, to 
determine the commensurate level of E&S due diligence (for example, environmental and social 
impact assessment (ESIA), environmental and social management plan (ESMP), codes of practice, 
etc.), the required plans (resettlement action plans, indigenous peoples plan, biodiversity action 
plan, etc.), as well as other safeguard instruments. 
34. 
Information disclosure. The overall E&S risk level of the programme is moderate risk which 
warrants disclosure of the ESMF document. Both the English and local language (Portuguese) 
versions of the ESMF document have been disclosed on the Accredited Entity’s website. 
Furthermore, the Accredited Entity commits to disclosing information about the project’s activities 
including E&S screening results, assessments, management plans, and monitoring reports in a 
timely manner and in local languages as much as possible. 
35. 
Key impacts and mitigation measures (citing requirements of triggered PS 2 - 8) Impacts and 
risks that may potentially result from the project are diverse in nature. The project may have 
environmental impacts on biodiversity due to project activities in or next to critical habitats or 
environmentally sensitive areas, use of natural resources and those resulting from the potential use 
of pesticides. Additionally, potential social impacts include land tenure, physical and/or economic 
displacement, cultural heritage and indigenous peoples. Management measures are included in the 
ESMF for potential risks and outlines for documents related to due diligence and management of 
E&S risks and impacts are included as annexes of the ESMF such as ESIA, ESMP and indigenous 
peoples plan. 
36. 
Capacity of executing entities. Implementation arrangements include a project team with 
E&S safeguards experts, responsible for monitoring and implementation of safeguards for the 
project. Capacity building and technical assistance, including training for project personnel, will be 
provided where required to ensure implementation of management plans. The Accredited Entity 
will maintain oversight on safeguards related matters. 
37. 
Monitoring and evaluation. The E&S monitoring, review and reporting requirements are 
presented in the ESMF. The indicative cost of $17 million related to ESMF implementation was also 
provided which seems to be high cost, reducing the amount of effective payments to the final 
beneficiaries.   
38. 
Stakeholders engagement process (consultations undertaken and plan for continuing 
engagement). Stakeholder consultations have been undertaken during the course of developing the 
project. These include engagement with the national REDD-PLUS committee which represents

=== PAGE 120 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 120 
 
 
 
 
 
diverse stakeholder groups. A stakeholder engagement plan will be prepared describing 
consultations and engagements that will be undertaken throughout the project implementation in 
alignment with the national requirements and guidelines.  
39. 
Project-level or institutional-level grievance redress mechanism. The project will employ a 2-
tier grievance redress mechanism consisting of a project-level grievance redress mechanism, and 
the Accredited Entity’s institutional-level grievance redress mechanism which can be used when 
complainants are not satisfied with the response from the former. Information on the grievance 
redress mechanism will be communicated in stakeholder engagement activities planned for the 
project. 
3.2 
Gender policy 
40. 
The AE has analysed gender equality and women’s empowerment issues in the period of the 
achievement of results by comparing its social and environmental standards against diagnostic 
questions and review indicators. Three projects which contributed to the results achieved, that is, 
the going green, catalysing the contribution of indigenous lands to the conservation of Brazil’s 
forest ecosystems (GEF project) and amazon portal seeds projects were reviewed for alignment 
against these indicators. The alignment review points out that these projects did not directly 
address gender equality and women’s empowerment, though women participated in the some of 
the projects’ activities. For example, in the amazon portal seeds project women participated in seed 
collection and in project’s management councils. Further, stakeholder engagements encourage 
female representation in the thematic advisory chamber on safeguards established by CONAREDD-
PLUS. However, while the stakeholder engagement report included in the proposal indicates that 
consultative processes have increasingly prioritized the participation of local communities it is not 
clear whether equitable representation of both men and women was ensured and how 
participation contributed to the implementation of these projects for the benefit of both men and 
women.  
41. 
Having seen that there are caveats in addressing the concerns and needs of women in the 
REDD-PLUS projects, the AE recommended the integration of gender issues in REDD-PLUS project 
design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation including conducting gender analyses, 
collection of sex-disaggregated baseline data, and defining relevant gender and social inclusion 
indicators and targets. The assessment by the AE determined the alignment of the projects 
(undertaken for the achieved results) and national policies and regulations with its Social and 
Environmental Standards (SES). The (SES) includes an overarching principle of gender equality and 
women’s empowerment.  
42. 
Recommendations on gender-related issues have been taken up in the preparation of the 
proposal for the use of proceeds. The proposal contains a gender assessment; therefore, it complies 
with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. The AE prepared a gender 
assessment based on desk review of available information which has gathered evidence on gender 
issues in the context of the country, the forestry sector and REDD-PLUS. The gender assessment 
also highlights the need to empower women through the implementation REDD-PLUS by 
mainstreaming gender in the design, monitoring and evaluation of activities. Some of the entry 
points presented by the project to promote equal participation and access of project benefits 
include, representation of men and women in project governance arrangements and identifying and 
addressing gender inequalities in the country’s land tenure system. Findings in the gender 
assessment will be re-assessed and revised as needed as part of the environmental and social 
impact assessment (ESIA) of the project. 
43. 
The AE has provided a preliminary gender action plan which translates some of the 
recommendations in the gender assessment into actions corresponding to the project’s outputs, 
indicators with sex-disaggregated targets, and institutions responsible for implementing the actions

=== PAGE 121 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 121 
 
 
 
 
 
are also included. The AE will collect sex-disaggregated baseline data during the ESIA process. 
Baseline information collected on gender-related issues will be used to inform the gender action 
plan and rationalise sex-disaggregated targets contained therein. The project’s cost for 
implementing the activities listed in gender action plan will be based on resources allocated to the 
project by GCF – the AE is recommended to avail sufficient financial resources for the 
implementation of these activities. Additionally, the AE is strongly recommended to incorporate 
timelines in the gender action plan based on the duration of implementation of subprojects. The 
project will engage the services of a gender specialist who will be responsible for gender 
mainstreaming and ensuring the implementation of the gender action plan.  
3.3 
Risks  
44. 
Overall Program assessment (medium risk):  
(a) 
Under the Pilot Programme for REDD-plus results-based payments, GCF is requested to 
provide a payment of USD 96,452,228 for the emission reductions achieved by Brazil in 
2014 - 2015. The volume of ERs for which GCF funding is sought is within 30% of the total 
payable volume as required under the ToR.  The proceeds will be used to strengthen the 
implementation of Brazil’s ENREDD-plus and reinvest in the pilot for environmental 
services incentive programme, as permitted under the ToR.  
45. 
AE / EE capability to execute the current programme (low risk): 
(a) 
The project will be implemented under UNDP’s Direct Implementation modality (DIM). 
Therefore, UNDP will be an AE and EE for this project. UNDP is an accredited entity and has 
an extensive track record in implementation of the projects in developing countries. As an 
EE for the project, UNDP is responsible and accountable for managing this project, including 
monitoring and evaluation, M&E of project interventions and achieving project outcomes. 
The Ministry of Environment of Brazil (MMA) will be UNDP’s government partner and 
support technical oversight and participate in the Project Board.   
46. 
Program specific execution risks (medium risk): 
(a) 
Risk of double payments: The FP indicates that ‘Info Hub Brasil’, an online tool for national 
repository, has been developed to ensure full transparency of the information and avoid 
double payments for the same result. The government issued a Federal Decree for 
coordinating and monitoring the implementation of the ENREDD-PLUS in Brazil. According 
to the national legislation, only four entities may receive REDD-plus RBP and the results 
paid for are registered in the national repository (Info Hub Brasil).  
(b) 
Use of proceeds: Brazil will use the proceeds from results-based payments to pilot an 
environmental services incentive programme (Floresta+) and strengthen the 
implementation of Brazil’s ENREDD-PLUS strategy focusing on the governance system. 
Thus, the end use of proceeds is as permitted in the ToR. The proposed activities will be 
implemented over six years and thus need to be adequately monitored to achieve the 
desired impact.   
(c) 
Operation modality: The Output 1 of the project aims to provide incentives for 
environmental services in accordance with Brazil’s Forest Code, ENREDD-PLUS and Brazil’s 
NDC. It will use direct payments and operate through 4 modalities. However, the guidelines, 
duration, and institutions that will manage funds for Modality 3 and 4 need to be 
determined during the project implementation. Also, the operation of a governance 
structure and the definition of values and priority areas for direct payments are yet to be 
finalized. Given that more than 85% of the budget is going to Output 1 according to the 
indicative project financing, it is recommended that AE closely coordinate with relevant

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 122 
 
 
 
 
 
implementing partners for timely decision making on the currently undetermined areas 
mentioned earlier and monitor the progress on the use of proceeds.   
(d) 
Disbursement plan: The project implementation will be over 6 years and the FP provided 
information that how AE fee and Project Management Cost (PMC) will be used over such 
period. The AE has identified a political risk that may impact the implementation during 
initial months. It is recommended that the disbursement of GCF’s financing is done over the 
six years period corresponding to the implementation of the activities. 
47. 
Compliance risk (medium risk): 
(a) 
UNDP is the AE on this program and is well aware of the contractual obligations placed 
upon it as AE to ensure that appropriate AML/CFT and due diligence processes are 
implemented.  Nevertheless, Brazil has experienced major incidents of corruption in recent 
years, including its energy sector, so it is especially important that appropriate safeguards 
be implemented.  Brazil prosecutors have instituted a number of high profile anti-
corruption cases which evidences some serious attempts to rein in corruption and address 
money laundering.  Based on these experiences, Compliance assigns this a Medium risk 
rating. 
48. 
GCF’s portfolio concentration risk (low risk):  
(a) 
In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-
material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single 
proposal. 
49. 
Conclusion (medium risk): 
(a) 
It is recommended that any approval by the Board is made by considering the above points.

3.4 
Results monitoring and reporting 
50.

3.5 
Legal assessment  
52. 
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 5 August 
2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016. 
53. 
The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 123 
 
 
 
 
 
54. 
The proposed project will be implemented in the Federative Republic of Brazil, country in 
which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, 
GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be 
further assessed. The Secretariat submitted a draft of the privileges and immunities agreement to 
the Government of Brazil on 8 December 2017.  
55. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit 
(IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities 
and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant 
privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made 
only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the 
country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities. 
 
IV. 
List of proposed conditions (including legal) 
56. 
In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject 
to the following conditions:  
i. 
Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the 
Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and 
ii. 
Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

Section B: Carbon elements 
B.1. Forest Reference Emission Level / Forest Reference Level (FREL/FRL) 
Criteria 
Score 
Remarks 
(i) Is the FREL/FRL consistent with the GHG inventory, including the 
definition of forest used? 
1 
The AT notes that, overall, the FREL maintains consistency, in terms of 
sources for the activity data and the emission factors, with the GHG 
inventory included in Brazil’s national communication. 
The AT considers the treatment of non-CO2 gases as an area for future 
technical improvement to maintain consistency with the GHG 
inventory included in the national communication.  
(ii) Is the FREL/FRL based on historical data and is it equal to or below 
the average annual historical emissions during the reference period, 
unless a country is an HFLD country? 
2 
The FREL is based entirely on historical data and represents the mean 
annual emissions from gross deforestation in the Amazonia biome for 
the period 1996–2010. 
(iii) Is the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in Decision 
12/CP.17? 
2 
The AT concluded that the information used by Brazil in constructing 
its FREL for deforestation in the Amazonia biome is transparent and 
complete and is in overall accordance with the guidelines for 
submission of information on FRELs (as contained in the annex to 
decision 12/CP.17). 
(iv) Is the data and information provided for the FREL/FRL transparent? 
(has information been provided to allow an understanding of how 
UNFCCC guidance on submission of information on reference levels has 
been addressed?) 
2 
The assessment team notes that the data and information used by 
Brazil in constructing its FREL are transparent and complete 
(v) Is the FREL/FRL complete?  (has information been provided that 
allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?) 
2 
The AT considers that the information used by Brazil in constructing 
its FREL for deforestation in the Amazonia biome is transparent and 
complete. And allows the reconstruction of the FREL. 
The AT noted that the transparency and completeness of information 
improved significantly in the modified FREL submission, without the 
need to alter the approach or values used to construct the FREL and

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 126 
 
 
 
 
 
commends Brazil for the efforts made. The new information provided 
in the modified submission, including through the data made available 
on websites and the examples on how estimates of CO2 emissions from 
deforestation were calculated, increased the reproducibility of FREL 
calculations. 
(vi) Is the FREL/FRL consistent? (were data and methodologies applied 
consistently over the time series used for the construction of the 
FREL/FRL?) 
1 
The AT indicated that information provided for the years 1996 and 
1997 is less accurate (as the data were not based on measured data) 
and less consistent over the time series compared to the time series 
constructed from 1998 onwards. This is because digital information on 
deforestation was available only from 1998 onwards (aggregated for 
the period 1998–2000, and on a yearly basis thereafter), while data for 
1996 and 1997 are only available in analogue format. In response to 
this observation, Brazil indicated that it is seeking to complete the 
annual digital time series with data from 1996 to 2000, and that this 
will improve the accuracy of the estimates provided in the submission 
for the years 1996 and 1997 as well as for the individual years 1998, 
1999 and 2000. Overall, the AT considered that a better estimation of 
deforestation estimates for the years 1996–1997 (i.e. through the 
digitalization of the deforestation maps) is an area for future technical 
improvement.  
In the FP, Brazil indicates that since the assessment of the FREL, Brazil 
received inputs from various international stakeholders, with the 
general understanding that historical information before year 2000 
should not be used in a future national FREL submission from Brazil to 
the UNFCCC in 2020.  
The FP indicates that Brazil’s efforts now are focused on building 
deforestation data for all Brazilian biomes to scale up REDD-plus to the 
national level, rather than improving the historical data before year 
2000 for the Amazon biome. 
(vii) Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The data and methodologies used neither 
over- nor under-estimate emissions and/or removals during the reference 
period, so far as can be judged) 
1 
As discussed above, the AT noted that estimates of deforestation for 
the years 1996–1997 are less accurate than the rest of the time series. 
The AT considers that a better estimation of estimates for the years 
1996–1997 may be achieved through digitalization of the deforestation

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 127 
 
 
 
 
 
maps. In addition, the AT recommended continuation of the 
improvements of the carbon map and note the already ongoing effort 
the ongoing effort on monitoring complex forest cover change 
dynamics and small deforestation events.  
(viii) Have all REDD-PLUS activities that are a significant source of 
emissions been included? 
1 
The AT acknowledges that Brazil included the most significant activity 
in the FREL (reducing emissions from deforestation).  The AT notes 
that other activities could also be significant in the Amazonia biome, in 
particular degradation. The AT notes that the current exclusion of 
degradation from the FREL appears to be conservative and 
acknowledges the intention expressed by Brazil to: (i) continue 
monitoring forest degradation to assess whether the reduction of 
deforestation is leading to an increase in forest degradation activities 
and (ii) include emissions from degradation in future FREL 
submissions when new, adequate data and better information become 
available. 
The AT considered the exclusion of degraded areas conservative in the 
context of constructing the FREL. 
(ix) Have all of the most significant pools been included?  
 
 
1 
The AT acknowledges that Brazil included in the FREL the most 
significant pools in terms of emissions from forests. However, the AT 
noted that changes in soil carbon can be significant when forest land is 
converted to grassland or cropland. Brazil explained in its submission 
that it does not have data on the dynamics of forest conversion for all 
years in the period considered in the REL. However, Brazil mentioned 
two sources of information that were used as proxies to estimate the 
fate of soil carbon during forest conversion to other uses. The AT 
considers that the exclusion of soil organic carbon is adequately 
justified by Brazil and commends the efforts to obtain better 
information on this pool in the future with the aim of including it as 
part of the step-wise approach. 
The AT also requested clarification on the reasons for the omission of 
the dead wood pool. The AT considers that when deforestation occurs, 
there are emissions from dead wood to the atmosphere that would 
need to be estimated.  The AT considers the treatment of emissions 
from dead wood (i.e. the inclusion of this pool or the provision of more

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 128 
 
 
 
 
 
information justifying its omission) as an area for future technical 
improvement of the FREL. 
 The AT noted that the current omissions of pools and gases is likely to 
be conservative in the context of the FREL. 
(x) Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included?  
1 
The FREL includes CO2 emissions The AT notes that the exclusion of 
non-CO2 gases is justified by Brazil and is likely to be conservative in 
the context of the FREL. Nevertheless, the AT identified the treatment 
of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement. 
(xi) Is the information provided in the construction of the FREL/FRL 
(data, methodologies and estimates) guided by the most recent applicable

For the construction of the FREL of the Amazonia biome, Brazil used

the exclusion of de dead wood pool (see ix). 
(xiii) What is the ref period for the FREL/FRL? 
2 
Reference period is 14 years (1996-2010).

2 
Brazil has submitted FREL A and FREL B to the UNFCCC. FREL B was

The elaboration and implementation of specific policies related to

and provided recommendations to address the challenges for 
example, improving access to dispute resolution, improving 
demarcation and regularizing tenure, enhancing capacities, and

particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions 
referred to in paragraphs 70 and 72 of this decision. 
Pass 
The governance structure of the PPCDAm involves representation of 
civil society, indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities 
in the CONAREDD+. The thematic advisory boards serve as fora and 
platform for social participation on the ENREDD+ with specific 
criteria of participation and representation. This is complemented at 
the project level with the guidelines from BNDES related to 
involvement of indigenous and traditional communities, requirement 
for consent, land acquisition and resettlement and conservation areas. 
Promoting direct access to benefits was an identified challenge related 
to this safeguard which is addressed by mobilizing resources from 
other projects that support the communities.    
(v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests 
and biological diversity, ensuring that the actions referred to in paragraph 
70 of this decision are not used for the conversion of natural forests, but 
are instead used to incentivize the 12 protection and conservation of 
natural forests and their ecosystem services, and to enhance other social 
and environmental benefits.

protected areas from 2011 to 2015 including support to the 
implementation of 114 conservation units. There were also policies 
that were implemented that fostered conservation and sustainable 
use of biodiversity and natural resources. The SOI identified several 
challenges in addressing and respecting this safeguard that would 
need to be addressed in succeeding actions.

reduction results.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP100 
Proposal name: 
REDD+ results-based payments for results achieved by 
Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015 
Accredited entity:

I. 
Scorecard application
1. 
The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), with the support of a land use, land-
use change and forestry (LULUCF) expert selected from the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) roster of experts and with experience in REDD-plus 
assessment and analysis, assessed the proposal “REDD+ results-based payments for results 
achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015” using the scorecard included in the 
terms of reference for the REDD-plus results-based payments (RBP) pilot programme.
1.1 
Summary of scorecard results

All criteria “pass”

of 
proceeds 
and 
non-carbon 
benefits 
Score: 2 
2.
Based on the above results and following the terms of reference procedures, the TAP
recommends that the Board consider the following:
(a)
GCF volume of emissions reductions: 18,819,946.91 tonnes of carbon dioxide
equivalent (tCO2eq);
(b)
Additional 2.5 per cent for use of proceeds and non-carbon elements; and
(c)
Proposed REDD-plus RBP (USD 5/tCO2eq): USD 96,452,228.
II.
Main findings
3.
The following paragraphs describe the main findings on those sections of the scorecard
where the assessment was found to be lower than the maximum score for each criterion (see
details in annex):
(a)
B.1.(i) Consistency between the Forest Reference Emissions Level/Forest
Reference Level- FREL/FRL and the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory: the
Assessment Team (AT) considered the treatment of non-CO2 gases as an area for future
technical improvement to maintain consistency with the GHG inventory, since the GHG

=== PAGE 140 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 140 
 
 
inventory included in the second national communication reports non-CO2 GHG 
emissions from biomass burning after forest conversion to agricultural uses for the 
period 1990–2005 and the FREL excludes emissions from non-CO2 GHG (see paragraph 
29 of Brazil’s FREL Technical Assessment Report-TAR). In its funding proposal, Brazil 
further states that the estimation of non-CO2 emissions from fire resulting from 
deforestation is expected to be improved in the next national GHG inventories. These 
estimates will be included in the national FREL to be submitted by Brazil to the UNFCCC 
in 2020 if consistency of the time-series can be assured and if deemed significant. 
Consequently, the score is 1. There is evidence that inconsistencies will be resolved in 
the next GHG inventory or FREL/FRL or justified; 
(b) 
B.1.(vi) Consistency of the FREL/FRL: the AT noted that the information provided for 
the years 1996 and 1997 is less accurate (as the data were not based on measured data) 
and less consistent over the time series compared to the time series from 1998 onwards. 
Overall, the AT considers that a better estimation of deforestation estimates for the 
years 1996–1997 (i.e. through the digitalization of the deforestation maps) is an area for 
future technical improvement (see paragraph 17 of Brazil’s TAR). In its funding 
proposal, Brazil responded to this observation by informing the AT that it was seeking 
financing to complete the annual digital time series with data from 1996 to 2000, and 
that this would improve the accuracy of the estimates provided in the submission for the 
years 1996 and 1997 as well as for the individual years 1998, 1999 and 2000. Since 
then, Brazil received inputs from various international stakeholders, with the general 
understanding that historical information before year 2000 should not be used in a 
future national FREL submission from Brazil to the UNFCCC in 2020. The score is 1. 
“Significant issues were raised and were resolved; or issues that were not resolved due 
to the limitation of time and data but were not material to the consistency of the 
FREL/FRL, and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome the 
limitation”; 
(c) 
B.1.(vii) Accuracy of the FREL/FRL: some issues were raised during the UNFCCC FREL 
Technical Assessment-TA that negatively affect the accuracy of Brazil’s FREL regarding 
the deforestation maps for 1996−1997, the “carbon map” used in the FREL, and the 
assumption of zero biomass after deforestation (paragraphs 17 and 37 (a), 19 and 13 of 
Brazil’s TAR). Brazil proposed measures to address these issues. The score is 1.  
“Significant issues were raised and were resolved; or issues that were not resolved due 
to the limitation of time and data but were not material to the accuracy of the FREL/FRL, 
and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome them”; 
(d) 
B.1.(viii) Inclusion of the most significant activities: in assessing the activities 
included in the FREL, the AT considered that degradation is a significant activity based 
on the estimates provided by Brazil (paragraph 39 of the TAR). The AT acknowledged 
(paragraph 31 of the TAR) the intention expressed by Brazil to: (i) continue monitoring 
forest degradation to assess whether the reduction of deforestation is leading to an 
increase in forest degradation activities (displacement of emissions); and (ii) include 
emissions from degradation in future FREL submissions when new, adequate data and 
better information become available. The score is therefore 1, meaning that not all 
REDD-plus activities that are a significant source of emissions have been included “but 
this is justified due to lack of data, and/or the omission does not overestimate emissions 
or underestimate removals, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include data 
in the future”; 
(e) 
B.1.(ix) Inclusion of the most significant pools: the AT identified the treatment of 
emissions from dead wood (i.e. the inclusion of this pool or the provision of more 
information on the justification of its omission) as an area for future technical 
improvement (paragraph 38 (a) of the TAR). In the funding proposal, Brazil presents an

=== PAGE 141 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 141 
 
 
assessment of the significance of the wood pool based on its FREL C (2018) submission 
and the comparison of its second and third national GHG inventories, concluding that 
Brazil considers that the dead wood pool is not a significant source of emissions and 
hence does not include it in the FREL. The score is 1 “No, but justified due to lack of data 
and/or the omission does not overestimate emissions or underestimate removals, 
noting that countries should indicate a plan to include data in the future”; 
(f) 
B.1.(x) Inclusion of all significant sources of emissions: the AT considered the 
treatment of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement to maintain 
consistency with the GHG inventory included in the national communication (see 
paragraph 31 of the TAR). In the funding proposal, Brazil points out that non-CO2 
emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome are not available for other years, and 
hence the recalculation of the emissions estimates to include non-CO2 emissions would 
not be possible at this point. Brazil further noted that the estimation of emissions from 
fire resulting from deforestation is expected to be improved in by next national GHG 
inventory, and if possible, non-CO2 emissions from fire will be included in the national 
FREL for the biomes where this is a significant source of emissions if the consistency of 
the time series can be assured and if deemed relevant. The corresponding score is 1. 
“No, but justified due to lack of data and/or the omission does not overestimate 
emissions, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include data in the future”; 
(g) 
B.1.(xi) Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change guidance applied: for the 
development of the FREL of the Amazon biome, Brazil used the methodology provided 
in the 2003 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Good Practice Guidance for 
Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (hereinafter referred to as the IPCC good 
practice guidance for LULUCF) as a basis for estimating changes in carbon stocks in 
forest land converted to other land-use categories (see paragraph 11 of the TAR). The 
score is therefore 1; 
(h) 
B.1.(xv) Aggregate FREL/FRL uncertainties: Aggregate uncertainties estimates were 
not included in the FREL submission. The score is therefore zero: “No information on 
uncertainties provided or > 50%”; 
(i) 
B.2.(v) Accuracy of data and information provided in the technical annex: The 
LULUCF experts noted that Brazil provided some information related to the 
uncertainties, although this information was not used in assessing the uncertainties of 
the emissions estimates. Thus, the effect of these uncertainties on the accuracy of the 
results from the implementation of the activity on reducing emissions from 
deforestation could not be assessed. Some accuracy issues were also raised regarding 
the assumption that forests are intact (based on the figures provided in the “carbon 
map”) before being deforested, which may imply a potential overestimation of 
emissions from deforestation. Despite this, and based on the assumptions used, the 
LULUCF experts concluded that the results are accurate to the extent possible 
(paragraph 33 of the Technical report on the technical analysis of the technical annex to 
the biennial update report (TATR)). The score is 1. “Significant issues were raised and 
were resolved; or issues that were not resolved due to the limitation of time and data 
but were not material to the accuracy of the results, and the country has provided a plan 
on how it will seek to overcome them”; and 
(j) 
B.2.(vii) Aggregate uncertainties for results: Brazil did not provide information on 
aggregate uncertainties. The score is zero. “No information on uncertainties provided or 
> 50%. Note: 0 = FAIL for REDD-plus results submitted for assessment from 2019 if no 
information on uncertainties has been provided”.

=== PAGE 142 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 142 
 
 
2.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
                    Scale: High 
4. 
Brazil reduced emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in the order of 
6,125,501,727.00 tCO2eq between 2006 and 2016 through different effective policies and 
measures. The Amazon biome comprises approximately 4,197,000 km2 and corresponds to 
49.29 per cent of the national territory (IBGE1, 2011). Brazil has six biomes, but the Government 
has concentrated its actions towards reducing deforestation in the Amazon and Cerrado Biomes. 
In 2006 the LULUCF sector represented 83 per cent of Brazil’s total CO2 emissions. In 2016 that 
number was reduced to 47.7 per cent. (Second national communication, MCTI2, 2016). Despite 
the absolute and relative reductions, the LULUFC sector still represents a significant source of 
emissions. 
5. 
In June 2014, Brazil submitted a dynamic FREL to be applied to emissions reduction 
results achieved in the period 2006−2010 (FREL A) and 2011−2015 (FREL B). Later in 
December 2014, Brazil submitted its first biennial update report (BUR) to the UNFCCC that 
contained an annex with REDD-plus results for the period 2006 to 2010, inclusive. The second 
BUR was submitted in February 2017 and included an annex with the emissions reduction 
results achieved in the Amazon biome in the period 2011 to 2015, based on FREL B, estimated 
as the mean in CO2 emissions from gross deforestation from the period 1996 to 2010. The 
current proposal accounts for only 2 per cent of the total volume of REDD-plus results between 
2014 and 2015 (measured against FREL B), equivalent to a total of 25,093,262.55 tCO2eq or 25 
MtCO2eq.  
6. 
The third BUR is expected to be submitted to the UNFCCC in 2019 and should include 
REDD-plus results up to 2017, measured against FREL C. After 2014 Brazil has continued its 
efforts to avoid deforestation mainly through command and control efforts. Over the past eight 
years, the average annual deforestation rate for the Legal Amazon region was around 6,300 km2, 
with a variation of around 1,000 km2. Brazil understands the importance of addressing the risk 
of reversal and has been doing that through various policies and initiatives.  
7. 
The FREL included only CO2 emissions. In the FREL submitted by Brazil to UNFCCC, the 
country recognizes the treatment of emissions of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical 
improvement of the FREL. Further improvement is also needed in understanding the difference 
between deforestation and forest degradation, which is increasingly being considered as an 
important source of emissions. There is a recognition of the need to continuously improve the 
GHG emissions estimates associated with REDD-plus activities, pools and gases.  
8. 
As noted in the scorecard, Brazil has not submitted the aggregate uncertainty estimates 
or a justification for not submitting them. Therefore, TAP is recommending further work in 
justifying aggregate uncertainty estimates. On the other hand, and as stated above, Brazil is 
placing an emphasis on the Cerrado and Amazon biomes, and the intention is to extend the 
REDD+ efforts at the national level. However, there is a need to provide further information on 
the activities Brazil is undertaking, other than monitoring, to avoid the displacement of 
emissions while the national level is reached. 
9. 
From a strategic level, Brazil’s National Policy on Climate Change and Forest Code 
provide the overarching guidelines for Brazil’s REDD-plus actions. The federal Action Plan for 
the Prevention and Control of Deforestation in the Legal Amazon (PPCDAm) has been a very 
important policy to pave the way for avoiding deforestation. The plan is in its fourth phase of 
implementation and is expected to last until 2020. 
                                                                
1 Instituto Brasileiro de Geografia e Estatistica- National Institute of Geography and statistics 
2 Ministério da Ciência, Tecnologia, Inovações e Comunicações - Ministry of Science, Technology, Innovation and 
Communication.

=== PAGE 143 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 143 
 
 
10. 
The methodologies provided for calculating emissions reductions are clear and robust, 
and the payment for 2014 results seem accurate according to the scorecard. The Government of 
Brazil is transitioning from a command and control strategy to promoting a strategy that 
involves incentives for conservation. In this context, the use of the proceeds will need to be 
revisited in the future, understanding that there is a possibility of changes to the policies that 
could potentially affect the drivers of deforestation in a complex political dynamic. However, in 
the view of TAP, Brazil has shown a strong willingness to reduce deforestation in the past and 
has robust policies and strategies as well as institutions in place as to continue its positive trend 
to reduce deforestation.  
2.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
11. 
Gains in forest conservation from 2006 to 2015 in Brazil have been recognized as a 
success in a country that could change the global levels of CO2 emissions due to land-use change 
and deforestation. The different policies, the information systems that the country has put in 
place and the command and control strategies have demonstrated their success. In the last four 
years the deforetation rates have started to increase as per the National Institute for Space 
Research-INPE estimates, as follows: 
(a) 
August 2013 to July 2014: 5,012 km2; 
(b) 
August 2014 to July 2015: 6,207 km2; 
(c) 
August 2015 to July 2016: 7,893 km2; 
(d) 
August 2016 to July 2017: 6,947 km2; and 
(e) 
August 2017 to July 2018: 7,900 km2. 
12. 
Brazil understands the importance of addressing the risk of reversal and is promoting a 
long-term reduction of GHG emissions within the scope of REDD-plus actions, thus ensuring 
consistent and continuous results. To this end, several federal laws and instruments are in place 
including the Forest Code (which applies to the Amazon biome), which stipulates that rural 
landholdings must conserve 80 per cent of their area with native vegetation cover as a legal 
reserve; this applyies to landholdings located in originally forested areas. Furthermore, the 
PPCDAm includes a host of actions to monitor, analyse and improve coordinated actions for 
maintaining reduced deforestation rates. 
13. 
The use of the proceeds of the RBP received by Brazil from the GCF will be used to 
ensure the national REDD-plus strategy (ENREDD+) objective to contribute to climate change 
mitigation by eliminating illegal deforestation, promoting conservation and restoration of forest 
ecosystems, and fostering a low-carbon and sustainable forest economy, while delivering 
environmental, social and economic benefits as well as supporting the implementation of the 
nationally determined contribution (NDC).  
14. 
Particularly, the use of the proceeds will be used to support the development of a pilot 
Environmental Services Incentive Program for Conservation and Recovery of Native Vegetation 
(Floresta+) and strengthen the implementation of Brazil’s ENREDD+ through improvements in 
its governance structure and systems. 
15. 
The rationale to start supporting incentives derives from different studies that suggest 
that command and control measures have a limit and that there is a need to give positive 
incentives to people that contribute to forest conservation. 
16. 
Floresta+ is a new and innovative pilot programme that aims to provide incentives for 
environmental services in the Legal Amazon region, in accordance with Brazil’s Forest Code, the 
ENREDD+ and Brazil’s NDC. The programme targets small farmers, indigenous communities, 
traditional peoples and communities and public institutions/agencies (including states and

=== PAGE 144 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 144 
 
 
municipalities), civil associations, cooperatives and private law foundations that act on topics 
related to conservation and the recovery of native vegetation.  
17. 
The programme will work in priority areas based on a selected criterion and has the 
following specific objectives:  
(a) 
Provide monetary compensation to incentivize native vegetation conservation and 
recovery and improvement of ecosystems that generate environmental services 
(including but not limited to carbon); 
(b) 
Prevent the occurrence of deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires through 
financial incentives; 
(c) 
Incentivize the conservation and recovery of native vegetation of rural properties, 
conservation areas, indigenous lands, land settlements, and lands of traditional people 
and communities; 
(d) 
Promote compliance with the environmental legislation, especially that related to the 
protection and recovery of native vegetation (Forest Code); and 
(e) 
Offer a financial mechanism to foster the development and implementation of public 
policies aimed at conservation and the recovery of native vegetation. 
18. 
The Floresta+ pilot program will operate through resource distribution modalities to 
promote conservation actions, recovery of areas, support to associations and indigenous 
peoples and innovative actions for conservation and recovery of native vegetation. Additional 
criteria and priority areas will be developed and refined in consultation with relevant 
stakeholders, including indigenous peoples and traditional peoples and communities, civil 
associations, state government representatives, federal agencies and non-governmental 
organizations, among other things. 
19. 
Ten percent of the resources will be used to improve the REDD-plus elements to 
strengthen the implementation of ENREDD+, with a stronger governance structure and more 
transparent data and information systems, supporting the following activities: 
(a) 
Expansion of the forest monitoring system and measurement, reporting and verification 
(MRV) to include additional REDD-plus activities, pools and gases, considering the 
mapping products produced under the Brazilian Biomes Environmental Monitoring 
Program, for all biomes, as appropriate, following the guidance from the Working Group 
of Technical Experts on REDD+. The aim is to submit a national FREL to the UNFCCC by 
2020; 
(b) 
Development of a tool to monitor and measure the impacts of REDD-plus policies and 
investments and inform decision-making regarding the forest component of Brazil’s 
NDC;  
(c) 
Improvement of Brazil’s Safeguards Information System for REDD+ (SISREDD+) and its 
ombudsman, making it more complete, transparent and accessible; 
(d) 
Enhancement of the capacities and access of the various stakeholders for participating 
in the National REDD+ Committee (CONAREDD+) and its Consultative Chambers, 
including the revision of the National REDD+ Strategy in 2020; and 
(e) 
South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change designed by the 
Ministry of the Environment (MMA) and the Brazilian Cooperation Agency of the 
Ministry of Foreign Affairs. (ABC/MRE) 
20. 
The activities selected to be funded with the use of the proceeds are well thought-out. 
The strategy to use both command and control measures and to give conservation incentives 
through programmes like Floresta + is perceived by TAP as a paradigm shift. TAP understands

=== PAGE 145 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 145 
 
 
that there could be a risk in change in policies under the new government, but is confident that 
the use of the proceeds as proposed will continue to support Brazil’s conservation objectives. 
21. 
The development of the National REDD+ Strategy in Brazil has had a long history of 
broad stakeholder engagement. The Federal Decree 8576/ 2015 created CONAREDD+ for 
coordinating and monitoring the implementation of ENREDD+. The development of ENREDD+ 
included broad stakeholder engagement, including strong interactions between ENREDD+ and 
the Indigenous Committee on Climate Change. In 2016, CONAREDD+ established the Thematic 
Advisory Chamber on Safeguards (CCT Safeguards), which develops inputs to support the 
decision-making of the CONAREDD+ on issues related to monitoring how the Cancun 
Safeguards (decision 1/CP.16) are being addressed and respected in Brazil’s implementation of 
REDD-plus. CCT-Safeguards is coordinated by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the National 
Council of Extractive Populations and is composed of 30 members. Participants in CONAREDD+ 
and the Advisory Chamber of Safeguards are numerous, diverse and seek to be representative of 
stakeholders. 
22. 
The  proposal presents a preliminary gender analysis including relevant policies that are 
in place in Brazil, as well as gender gaps and challenges.  Based on the analysis, the proposal 
also presents a preliminary action plan with suggested entry points and indicators to measure 
and track progress on these actions.   
2.3 
Sustainable development potential  
 
      
        Scale: High 
23. 
The Amazon biome is the largest repository of biodiversity in the world. Conserving its 
vast biodiversity contributes to the Aichi Biodiversity Targets in several ways. After 2004, the 
Amazon biome has also recovered secondary forests, which enhanced carbon stocks due to the 
reduction of forest pressure with additional environmental benefits to the local and global 
communities. The TerraClass data indicates that on average, 22 per cent of the total deforested 
area before 2004 had been replaced by secondary vegetation in the subsequent years.  
24. 
The Amazon biome also supports the water cycle in Brazil and the South American 
continent with “aerial rivers”. Through the process of transpiration, trees ensure water enters 
our atmosphere and in turn produces clouds and rain. The planet’s land vegetation recycles 48 
cubic millilitres of water each day. A tenth of that is released by the Amazon rainforest alone –
more than the daily discharge of the Amazon River.3 
25. 
The economic value of the ecosystems services provided by the Amazon forests have not 
been fully estimated. For example, regulating rain for a country and therefore ensuring water 
provision for consumption and agriculture is by itself a huge economic value that surpasses 
traditional estimates like gross domestic product. The value of the “standing forest” needs to be 
valued in a much more comprenhensive manner. Accounting for traditional economic measures 
will innevitably imply transaction costs associated with opening new areas for agriculture. 
Brazil has significantly increased these transations costs through command and control 
measures, including  the restriction of access to credits and other measures that have worked 
effectively. However, decoupling the production of agricultural commodities from deforestation 
is still a major challenge. 
26. 
Moreover, according to the project proposal in annex VIII, the large reduction in 
deforestation in the Amazon region through the implementation of REDD-plus policies and 
measures has had a positive impact in the region. Since 2000, the Human Development Index 
for the Amazon region has improved faster than for the rest of Brazil.  
                                                                
3 Wright, Fu et all. Rainforest-initiated wet season onset over the Southern Amazon. Proceedings from the National 
Academy of Sciences of the United States of America. July 2017.

=== PAGE 146 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 146 
 
 
27. 
PPCDAm and REDD-plus policies have helped provide alternative livelihoods for forest-
dependent people. The use of the proceeds in the Floresta+ programme will be used to create an 
Environmental Services Incentive Program for Conservation and Recovery of Native Vegetation 
that targets indigenous peoples and traditional peoples and communities. This will allow for a 
win-win situation by supporting livelihoods and protecting environmental services at the local 
and regional levels.  
2.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
28. 
Brazil has taken leadership in REDD-plus activities under the forestry-related 
opportunities created by the UNFCCC and the Warsaw Framework for REDD-plus. The NDC of 
Brazil plays a major role in policies at country level and is also a game changer for global carbon 
emission. 
29. 
Maintaining the Amazon biome requires large-scale investment. According to the project 
proposal, a variety of finance sources, both national (federal, state and municipal-level public 
budgets, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), private sector) and international (donor 
countries, multilateral organizations, NGOs, private sector) have contributed directly and 
indirectly to the Brazilian Government’s efforts to reduce deforestation in the Amazon biome. 
Since 2006, Brazil has invested large amounts of its own national budget for this purpose. The 
Amazon Fund also made significant investments that have directly and indirectly contributed to 
the achievement of REDD-plus results in Brazil. 
30. 
The current economic crisis of Brazil has significanlty reduced government expenditure. 
The total budget executed by MMA from 2014 to 2017 was on average BRL 2.5 billion (USD 750 
million) per year, which corresponds on average to 0.11 per cent of total yearly public 
expenditure. A large proportion of this budget is spent on activities related to forest protection, 
especially in the Amazon biome. The national budget cuts from 2014 onwards were followed by 
an increase in deforestation rates in 2016. The economic crisis and budget allocations require 
innovative forms of climate finance both internally and with the support of the intenational 
cooperation.  
31. 
The task to support command and control activities in such a vast area requires lots of 
investments, including for on-the-ground teams, helicopters, vehicles and better information 
systems and data. Moreover, studies have shown that the linear relationship between fines and 
reductions in deforestation have an intrinsic limit on the fight against illegal deforestation. 
Investments in positive incentives are key to maintaining and further expanding upon low 
deforestation rates.  
32. 
The Floresta+ pilot programme is well designed to test a new approach that combines 
both carrots and sticks. Therefore, the use of the proceeds to test the combibed model and to 
advance in the fight against deforestation is essential, especially in the light of the economic 
crisis and the need to support the continuation of policies to avoid deforestation under the new 
government. 
33. 
This funding proposal came to GCF Secretariat as a response to the Board’s call for 
projects under the REDD-plus pilot programme. Brazil is presenting an accurate plan to invest 
the proceeds that are much needed.  
2.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
34. 
In the case of Brazil, REDD-plus implementation has been country-driven. The PPCDAm 
has been Brazil’s core REDD-plus policy for the Amazon region and has been revised in 4 
ocassions to make it more efficient. The MMA has been responsible for the coordination of the 
PPCDAm.

=== PAGE 147 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 147 
 
 
35. 
The PPCDAm is in full consistency with the ENREDD+ and Brazil’s NDC to the UNFCCC 
and the Paris Agreement. REDD-plus is a means for the implementation of Brazil’s NDC in the 
forest sector. The resources from GCF REDD-plus payments though the Floresta+ pilot 
programme are in alignment with PPCDAm and ENREDD+.  
36. 
This project proposal has had the approval by CONAREDD+ and will be implemented by 
theMMA with support from the United Nations Development Programme. The MMA has been 
fully involved in the preparation of this funding proposal, leading discussions on the use of 
proceeds as well as providing all input necessary to inform sections on policies and actions 
implemented that lead to emissions reductions.  
37. 
At project inception, a comprehensive and participatory Environmental Social Impact 
Assessment (ESIA) will be conducted once the design of the four modalities of the Floresta+ 
pilot programme is finalized. A series of modality-specific management plans will be developed 
with stakeholders and as necessary, such that all appropriate mitigation measures are put in 
place before commencing any activity that has the possibility of causing adverse impacts.  
38. 
Brazil has been a pioneer in regulatory and institutional arrangements for the 
monitoring and conservation of the Legal Amazon’s tropical rainforests. The Forest Code of 
2012 demonstrates Brazil’s sovereign commitment to the protection of native vegetation and 
the integrity of the climate system for the well-being of present and future generations. This law 
establishes administrative restrictions on the use of certain areas of native vegetation within 
private properties. In the Legal Amazon the law estipulates that rural landholdings must 
conserve 80% of their area with native vegetation cover, as a legal reserve, applying to 
landholdings located in originally forested areas. 
39. 
The National Policy on Climate Change (PNMC) (Law No. 12.187/2009) outlines the 
objectives and guidelines for addressing climate change in Brazil, using the force of law to 
pursue the national voluntary commitment of reducing GHG emissions by 36.1 to 38.9 per cent 
in relation to projected emissions by 2020. With regard to specific REDD-plus actions, Brazil has 
made a commitment to achieve, by 2020, a reduction of 80 per cent in the rate of deforestation 
in the Amazon biome and 40 per cent in the Cerrado biome. 
40. 
At state level, the nine states in the Legal Amazon have structured plans for forest 
conservation and the recuperation of degraded land in alignment with the PPCDAm and the 
PNMC. 
41. 
Brazil has demonstrated experience in managing the REDD-plus programme with 
resources from the donor community and specifically the programme with the Norwegian 
Government. The GCF payment will build on these experiences with the new modality of the 
Floresta+ pilot programme. 
2.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
 Scale: Medium 
42. 
Brazil has presented the payment for results, accounting for only 2 per cent of the total 
volume of REDD-plus results between 2014 and 2015 (measured against FREL B), equivalent to 
a total of 25,093,262.55 tCO2eq or 25 MtCO2eq. The evaluation of the scorecard has reduced this 
amount to USD 96,452,228 using the relevant criteria. 
43. 
The effectiness of the use of proceeds could only be evaluated in the future, as the 
Floresta+ pilot programme is still under design. Floresta+ pilot programme funds will be 
managed by a national bank for modalities 1 and 2, and by institutions that best suit the needs 
of Modalities 3 and 4. These will be selected as part of the design of the Floresta+ programme. 
44. 
Direct payments under Foresta+ will be calculated based on the area (hectares) of native 
vegetation remnants and environmental liabilities to be recovered, according to the data in the 
National Rural Environmental Registry System (SICAR). The amount of payment per hectare

=== PAGE 148 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 148 
 
 
will be defined in norms to be published by the Project Advisory Committee and should 
consider:  
(a) 
The relationship between the preserved native vegetation area and the area of 
deforested, degraded or burnt native vegetation within the rural property limits; 
(b) 
The opportunity cost of land use in areas with alternative land use; and 
(c) 
The reference values of other programmes using incentives for environmental services, 
when applicable. 
45. 
The effectiveness of these incentives will depend on several factors, including the 
selection of grantees and the ability to scale up the programme.  
46. 
With regard to the question from TAP about the sustainability of Floresta+ after project 
completion, the answer was that the programme is rooted on financial and legal elements. 
UNDP argues that there are other sources of funding that could be used to support its 
implementation after the GCF project, including: (i) budgetary resources from the MMA and the 
Ministry of Agriculture; (ii) resources from the National Fund for the Environment, Climate 
Fund or the Amazon Fund; (iii) resources from environmental compensation and conversion of 
fines from IBAMA4, ICMBio5 and other environmental state agencies; and (iv) resources from 
private initiatives that benefit from environmental services, such as sanitation and energy 
companies. However, these estimates have not been presented to GCF, and therefore it is not 
possible to judge if they will be effective. 
47. 
With regard to legal elements, Brazil’s Forest Code (Law 12,651/2012) establishes that 
rural properties in the Amazon region shall preserve at least 80 per cent of its native vegetation. 
This law also foresees the creation of a programme of payments for environmental services in 
its article 41 (Forest Code). In December 2018, a decree was issued that regulates the quotas for 
environmental reserves (Decree 9,640/2018); this aims at easing the identification and 
transaction of forest activities on rural properties. This will facilitate the process of 
identification of areas of intervention, creating a basis for the expansion of the program in the 
whole country. 
48. 
Moreover, Floresta+ will be supported by the development and application of 
information technology for addressing the issue of deforestation over recent years. In 2010 the 
Brazilian Government made it mandatory for all rural properties to be mapped and registered 
through a government system known as CAR (Cadastro Ambiental Rural). This environmental 
registry of rural properties is now fully operational and has over 4 million properties registered. 
It is a powerful tool to support enforcement of environmental laws that is fully transparent. This 
tool will form the basis for the monitoring and evaluation of the Floresta+ Programme.  
49. 
In terms of efficiency, GCF does not dictate terms for RBP under REDD-plus, since it is at 
the discretion of the recipient of the RBP system to make best use of the proceeds. For future 
transactions to be properly assessed by the TAP, it would be important to receive better 
guidelines from the Board on the types of actions and indicators they would like to see on the 
use of proceeds for consolidating REDD-plus goals and achievements. 
50. 
The efficiency of this REDD-plus process strongly depends on preventing ongoing forest 
degradation in Brazil. As stated in the scorecard, Brazil needs to work on understanding 
uncertainties, forest degradation processes and the displacement of emissions. These three 
elements will be critical in accounting for a real estimate of emissions in the future.  
                                                                
4  Instituto Brasileiro do Meio Ambiente e dos Recursos Naturais- Brazilian Institute of Environment and 
Renewable Natural Resourcesfor Biodiversity Conservation 
5  Instituto Chico Mendes de Conservação da Biodiversidade – Chico Mendes Institute

=== PAGE 149 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.02 
Page 149 
 
 
III. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
51. 
The Brazil REDD-plus project proposal presents a good case for financing under the GCF 
REDD-plus pilot programme. However, because of its nature, the project proposal lacks 
sufficient information on the efficacy of the utilization of the proceeds for the TAP to make 
specific and decisive remarks. 
52. 
The iTAP recommends this project be approved by the Board. 
53. 
The iTAP recommends that Brazil and the AE give special attention to the following: 
(a) 
Consider further analysis on how aggregated uncertainties affect the results and means 
to improve the FRELs and reporting; 
(b) 
Taking into account that there could be significant emissions caused by forest 
degradation, consider the continuation of efforts to understand the effects of forest 
degradation in emissions reporting; 
(c) 
Consider including information for the future submission to UNFCCC on the actions that 
the country is taking to prevent or minimize the displacement of emissions; and 
(d) 
Ensure the sustainability of the Floresta+ pilot programme with a plan around 
scalability, financing and expected outcomes and indicators that goes beyond the initial 
six-year period.
